the `parent` role appears once the first child is approved; and the parent reaches that child through the role switcher into the **existing Student Dashboard**. **There is no Parent Dashboard.** **(1) `ChildApplication` (§7)** — one row per child, grouped by a request id, carrying the child's names, sex, `schooling_stage`, per-child `status`, the chosen duplicate match, a bounded rejection reason and a staff-only internal note. **The child `User` is created AT APPROVAL, not at submission**: creating it up front is exactly what forces the all-or-nothing bundle, and deferring it means **a rejected child leaves no `User` row and no `FamilyLink` at all**. **(2) TD-4.2 is narrowed** from the bundle to **one child**: approving one application creates or links that child, creates the `FamilyLink(approved)`, grants the `parent` role if absent, and audits — in one transaction, leaving other children in the request untouched. **The parent's own application is decided explicitly and separately and is NEVER inferred from the children's outcomes.** A rejected child has no link, so partial approval cannot expose one — the safety property is structural rather than enforced. **(3) Consent is captured at submission and materialised at approval (§4.1a).** `ConsentRecord.student_id` cannot be written before the student exists, so the application carries the decisions as given — `data_processing`, per-child `media_release`, **the consent text version in force AT SUBMISSION**, and the moment of agreement — and the records are created at approval with **the submission's values, not the approval's**. `legal.consent_text_version` is a Super-Admin-editable setting: taking the current value would record that a parent consented to text **they never saw**. **(4) Duplicate children are resolved by an ADMINISTRATOR, never automatically.** The server proposes candidates shown as **name + linked parent + reference code**; the administrator chooses *link* or *create*. There is no natural key for a child — no national identifier and no date of birth — and matching on names alone would eventually attach a child to the wrong family. **(5) `FamilyLink.relationship_type`** — `mother | father | legal_guardian`, no free text and no `other`: the platform asks only *may this person act for this child*, and a grandparent who acts legally **is** a legal guardian. **Multi-parent linking is permitted and is now stated**: uniqueness has always been on the **pair**, two approved links are two independent authorizations, and `granted_by_user_id` already records which parent consented. Refusing it would be worse for safeguarding, because the second adult would use the first one's login and the trail would name the wrong person. **(6) `User.reference_code`** — a short, stable, **non-personal** student identifier, **random rather than sequential** (a sequence leaks enrolment order and headcount), in an alphabet without `0/O` or `1/I/l` because it is read aloud. **It identifies; it never authorises** — a lookup by code runs exactly the same authorization as a lookup by id, or the code becomes a bearer token by accident. **(7) `User.schooling_stage`** — `pre_primary | primary | middle | high | post_secondary | not_in_school`. The three Categories map to schooling stage, and **this field INFORMS an administrator's placement decision: it never gates, filters, validates or auto-assigns a Category or Level, and no logic may refuse a placement on it or on any age derived from it.** The Owner's own case is why: *a girl older than the usual high-school age is still placed in اليافعون if she is still in high school, or if the administrator considers it appropriate* — any automated rule would refuse that placement. **A date of birth is deliberately NOT added**: age correlates with schooling stage and does not determine it, so the platform records the signal the decision uses rather than a proxy for it. **(8) Linking is restricted to accounts with no login identity.** §4.3 already says minors have no login, so **a minor is structurally a student account with no `UserIdentity`** — a test needing no birth date. An adult holds their own account and consents for themselves. **When a minor gains a login, existing parent links are NOT revoked automatically**: binding a first identity to a student with approved links raises a **non-blocking review item**, and an administrator decides. **(9) §5.4's Family Dashboard is removed** and `/dashboard/parent` leaves §14.1. **A parent's home is their first approved child's dashboard**, and the switcher's `ولي الأمر` group carries a persistent **«＋ تسجيل طفل»** action — a parent holding the role with no approved children still sees that group containing only that action. **(10) A minimal Student Dashboard ships** (identity block with the reference code, today's and upcoming sessions, basic information) so the flow is walkable end to end; Quran progress, grades and exams remain later milestones. It serves both contexts on one route, with **a persistent banner naming whose data is shown**. **(11) TD-2 is NOT amended — parent permissions stay read-only**, and the absence is deliberate: TD-2 grants a parent only *view linked child's data* and *take online exams*, and the second is unreachable while R58 disables them. **(12) Recorded: the CNDP declaration has not been filed.** The plan is MVP first, the compliance register in `docs/compliance/` maintained as decisions are taken, then the declaration prepared from what the finished system actually processes — under which **no field is ever justified by *"we will declare it later"***. **Guardianship verification** and **whether an applicant has a right to the actual rejection reason** are **pending legal/CNDP confirmation**; nothing here assumes an answer, and neither changes the data model if answered later.

**Revision 61 (Document Owner instruction — the الإدارة section is Super Admin only, 2026-08-11):** **(1) Every node under §14.1's `الإدارة` section is Super Admin only, as a SECTION rule rather than four separate decisions.** Three of the four already were — المهملات, التقويم الهجري, إعدادات المنصة; the fourth, **`/admin/branches` (الفروع والقاعات)**, was *read: Admin · write: Super Admin* under Revision 26 and now joins them. Stating it as a section rule is the point: written as four module entries, the fifth module added there inherits nothing and the divergence returns silently, which is exactly how `/admin/branches` came to be the odd one out. **Enforced by a test** asserting every module registered under that section carries Super-Admin-only roles, so a future addition cannot quietly ship with `STAFF`. **(2) Revision 26's read retention is NOT withdrawn, and the distinction is load-bearing.** `GET /admin/branches` stays readable by an Admin, branch-scoped. R26 retained it deliberately — *"operational work depends on it, since a `Group` references a Branch, a Level and a Room, so withdrawing read access would make Group management impossible"* — and that was **verified against the current code before this revision was drafted**: `hooks/use-scope-options.ts` calls it, and it feeds `/admin/groups`, `/admin/schedules`, `/admin/content` and every scope selector in the back office. Withdrawing the endpoint would leave a branch Admin unable to create a group, schedule a class or scope content, **with no error explaining why** — the selectors would simply come back empty. So three things are separated deliberately: the **screen and navigation node** are Super Admin only; the **endpoint as a selector feed** stays Admin-readable; every **write** stays Super Admin only. **Nothing an Admin could do before becomes possible after; one screen becomes unreachable.** **This is a visibility and management boundary, not a data boundary** — withholding the data from Admins as well is a larger decision requiring every branch selector in the back office to be re-fed, and should be taken knowing that cost rather than as a consequence of this one. **(3) R60 applies on top**: a Super Admin *working as* Admin does not see the section either, because the interface reads the active role.

**Revision 60 (Document Owner decision — the Active Role, 2026-08-11):** §2.1 says a person may hold several roles and switch between them *"via an account switcher in the header"*, and said nothing more; the switcher was therefore presentation over a token carrying every role at once, so a Super Admin working as مؤطِّرة still held Super Admin authority on every endpoint. **The Active Role becomes a real authorization context.** **(0) Framing, recorded first because it governs the rest: this is a SAFETY mechanism, not containment.** Switching is self-service and instant, so it cannot defend against a Super Admin who intends harm — and no design permitting instant switching could. It delivers what it was approved for: a Super Admin can **test the platform exactly as a Teacher, Admin or Student experiences it**, an accidental click while acting as مؤطِّرة cannot delete a branch, and staff working in a lesser role hold least privilege by default. **(1) `active_role` joins §7's enumerated claims.** When present the token is **already narrowed**: `roles[]` and `role_scopes[]` carry only that role's entry. The reason is mechanical — every authorization decision reads one of those two fields, so narrowing them at issue narrows **every** call site at once (103 references across 28 files) without editing any, and a service *cannot* consult an un-narrowed array because none exists in that request. `roles[]` stays derived from `role_scopes[]`, so filtering the input narrows both — which matters because fourteen sites read `roles[]` directly. **Absent = today's behaviour**, for single-role accounts and for tokens minted before this revision. **This deliberately differs from §4.3's rule that the active child is NEVER a claim**, and the difference is stated so the two are not later "harmonised": a child names *whose data*, switches constantly, and needs immediate revocation; a role names *which authority*, switches rarely, and is already re-read from live rows on every refresh. **(2) The invariant — `active_role` is always one of the account's live assignments** — is enforced at three existing depths: at issue (`/auth/switch-role` and `/auth/refresh` both re-read `UserBranchRole` and refuse otherwise), at refresh, and per request on high-risk paths via TD-12. **(3) Switching is `POST /auth/switch-role`**: one indexed query, one signature, no logout, no new session, no refresh-cookie change, and **no `active_role` column anywhere** — not on `User`, not on `RefreshToken`. **Concurrent devices differ by construction**, each holding its own token. **(4) Refresh is the load-bearing path and fails safe.** The client holds the access token in memory only and the switch navigates by full page load, so the token is re-acquired from `POST /auth/refresh` on the next page. Refresh therefore accepts a requested `active_role`, validates it against the rows it already reads, **returns the resulting `active_role` explicitly**, and when the requested role is gone **falls back to the most privileged still-valid assignment rather than silently restoring unrestricted multi-role authority**. **(5) TD-12 freshness becomes active-role aware** — without this, exactly the most dangerous endpoints would keep full Super Admin authority while everything else narrowed, which is the single largest risk this revision carries: `assertFreshActive` now checks that the **active** role is still assigned and returns roles and scopes narrowed to it, because six callers use that return value for further scope decisions. **(6) §4.2 is NOT amended** — *"scope resolves per role, never as a flat union"* stands; the array simply has one entry, and branch scoping survives because the retained entry keeps its own `branches`. Two consequences are stated so they are not mistaken for defects: `branchesForRole`'s `isSuperAdmin` short-circuit correctly stops applying, which is the mechanism by which Super Admin authority actually disappears; and a Super Admin acting as مؤطِّرة is refused at all 44 `isSuperAdmin` sites including the Trash's destructive verbs. **(7) §4.3's Student/Parent ambiguity is resolved by the active role** (Owner decision): acting as Parent uses Parent behaviour and requires `X-Active-Child-ID`; acting as Student uses the §4.3 self-access bypass. The two paths become reachable deliberately rather than by an accident of which roles an account holds. **(8) `active_role` is recorded on EVERY audit row**, in `detail` (no schema change), alongside — never instead of — `actor_user_id`: the account stays the accountable identity, and the role records the *capacity* in which the person acted, because *"the Super Admin deleted it"* and *"the Super Admin, working as مؤطِّرة, deleted it"* describe different events. **(9) `GET /me` keeps reporting every assigned role** plus the active one: the switcher's menu is built from it, so narrowing it would remove the ability to switch back. `/me` answers *what may this person become*; authorization answers *what is this person now*.

**Revision 59 (Document Owner decision — deletion authority across the platform, 2026-08-09):** Revision 52 closed with *"no permanent-delete action exists, and none may be added without a further revision"*; this **is** that revision. **(1) Permanent deletion becomes a Super Admin action**, `DELETE /admin/trash/{id}` — it destroys a soft-deleted record, its declared cascade children and its Trash entry in **one transaction**, writes a `trash.permanent_delete` audit row, and is asserted against live role rows in the service rather than by the `/admin/` prefix (TD-2). Revision 52's reasoning is superseded rather than dismissed: destruction is still a data-retention decision, BR-15's ninety-day window is still the **default** path and is unchanged, and no other role can reach the action — but *"wait ninety days"* is not an answer a platform can give to a safeguarding erasure request, and the retention rule exists to prevent *accidental and unaccountable* destruction, which an audited, confirmed, Super-Admin-only action is not. **Refusals are loud and coded**, as restore's are: `DEPENDENTS_EXIST` (a live row still references it, and the referencing record is named), `NOT_YET_SUPPORTED` (no destruction plan is written for that type — a type joins the purgeable set when its destruction is **written and tested**, never by inference), `ALREADY_PURGED` (only the tombstone remains, and removing it alone is what the caller receives). **An `EducationalContent` purge also removes its quarantined object**, because the bytes are the point of that entity and a destroyed row beside a surviving object is an orphan rather than a deletion. **(2) Every deliberate deletion reaches the Trash.** BR-15 already required this; what is new is the **test that distinguishes an obligation from a non-obligation**, whose absence let four deletions through: *a deletion a person deliberately performed gets its own Trash entry; rows removed as a consequence of that deletion do not, because the parent's snapshot describes them.* Un-enrolling a student (`Enrollment`), removing a Teaching Group member (`StudentTeachingGroup`), unassigning a Subject from a Level (`LevelSubject`) and unlinking content from a Session (`SessionContent`) were audited but **left no snapshot** — invisible to the one screen that exists to answer *what was deleted and by whom*, and §7's runbook names the first two among the rows a restoration must reinstate. **One of the four was a recorded DECISION, not an oversight, and is reversed rather than fixed.** `trash-coverage.integration.test.ts` carried an explicit exemption for `enrollment.service.ts`, reasoning that *un-enrolment is a membership ending, not a record being deleted; there is nothing to restore that re-enrolling does not do properly.* That was a defensible reading of TD-5, which soft-deletes the enrolment row and leaves every academic record intact. It is superseded by the Owner's rule that **anything soft-deleted by any role appears in the Trash** — and §7's restore runbook already named `Enrollment` among the rows a restoration must reinstate, rows it could not have found. The other three were genuine oversights. **A fifth defect, of a different kind:** replacing an exam's supervising staff **hard-deleted** `ExamStaff` rows that carry `deleted_at` — under TD-5 a row with that column is never destroyed by an ordinary write, and the hard delete also broke (3)'s restore, since a restored exam could not say who had been supervising it. It now reconciles exactly as `SessionStaff` and `UserBranchRole` do: tombstone what is no longer wanted, revive what returns. It gets **no** Trash entry, for the reason that follows. **`SessionStaff` reconciliation and `UserBranchRole` revocation are NOT deletions** and are stated so here: each is one field of an *update* expressed as a tombstone, and giving them entries would fill the screen with rows nobody deleted. **(3) `Exam` joins the restorable set** — its deletion cascades to exactly one child table, `ExamStaff`, whose reinstatement is a single well-defined statement, so it meets the existing *written and tested* standard. No other type moves. **(4) `HijriMonthStart` gains a deletion**, `DELETE /admin/hijri-calendar/{year}/{month}` — it was the **only** entity a Super Admin can create through the platform with *no deletion at all*: the `deleted_at` column existed, every read filtered on it, and nothing could ever set it, so a month recorded in error was permanent. Super Admin only, **TD-15 `version` required** (as a query parameter — a `DELETE` with a body is refused by proxies), soft delete with a `Trash` snapshot, and **restorable and purgeable both**, since the deletion cascades to nothing. **Only the LAST recorded month may be withdrawn** (`LATER_MONTH_RECORDED`, naming the blocker): the months are a contiguous sequence and §5.7's conversion walks it, so a hole would reach a reader as *missing Ministry data* rather than as the deletion that produced it — `assertOrdered`'s existing write invariant, applied to removal. Publication is deliberately **not** a second obstacle: §5.7 already treats a correction as returning a month to `draft`, so a withdrawal is the stronger form of something the specification permits, and what is refused is the hole rather than the visibility. **(5) Recorded, not closed: `content.quarantine-purge` does not exist.** Revisions 52 and 53 both assert that BR-15's window *is enforced by* that TD-7 job; there is no such queue and no such worker, `purge_after` is written on every tombstone and has never been read, and the window is therefore **documented, depended upon, and not in force**. Switching on automatic destruction of production records is the Document Owner's decision and not a consequence of adding a manual action, so until that job ships permanent deletion is **exclusively** the deliberate action in (1).

**Revision 58 (Document Owner decision — an Exam has a mode, and a physical exam is scheduled, 2026-08-09):** §4.6 said **digital exams only in MVP** with *"paper sittings prepared outside the platform"*, and that exams are *"created independently of strict calendar bounds"*. **Both are superseded here**, on the Owner's decision that exams are sat on the premises. `Exam` gains **`mode` (`physical | online`)**, fixed after creation, plus `description` and `academic_year_id` for both modes and, for a physical sitting, **`branch_id`, `room_id`, `start_time`, `end_time`, an optional `administrative_group_id` (null = the whole Level) and an `ExamStaff` join carrying one supervisor and any number of assistants** — the shape `CourseScheduleStaff` already uses. **The mode is a discriminator, not a flag:** each mode carries exactly the columns its own reality has, and CHECK constraints refuse a physical exam an access policy or an online one a room. **One entity rather than `Exam` + `ExamSitting`**, because a physical exam's paper is not in the platform at all — the row *is* the sitting, and there is no second fact to separate. **Exam independence is narrowed, not deleted**: an exam still needs no Course Schedule and no term boundary to exist, and `round` remains an optional non-restricting selector; what changes is that a physical exam carries its own date and clock window, because a sitting nobody can find is not organised. **`mode = online` is declared and refused** — offered disabled with a stated reason (§14.4) and given **no columns, no endpoints and no UI**, since a field with no behaviour behind it is a promise the platform has not made; when it arrives it needs an exam link, a selected-student audience, an opening and closing window, access rules and submission settings, and it becomes **one more branch of the same form**, never a second admin page. **Duration, maximum mark, organisational notes and exam status are deliberately NOT added**: the first is `end_time − start_time`, the second is a second answer to what `grading.display_scale` already owns (§4.6 scores are basis points of the exam's total) **— SUPERSEDED by Revision 81, which retired that global scale and made the maximum a required column on `Exam`; with no platform-wide scale there is no second answer for it to be**, the third is `description`, and the fourth is a lifecycle TD-1 does not define. **The §4.4c three-mode target is not reused**: an exam is sat by a roster at a premises and the Teaching Group split has no bearing on who sits a paper, so one optional Administrative Group is the honest shape. A physical exam appears in the shared calendar as **`kind: 'exam'`** with its own colour, used identically in the calendar, the list, the details dialog and the type indicator.

**Revision 57 (Document Owner decision — a Course Schedule carries its own name, 2026-08-09):** `RecurringCourseSchedule` had **no name**: a class was identified by its Subject and its target, which identifies correctly and names poorly — two classes in one Subject for one group could not be told apart at a glance, and R56's unified Scheduling screen made that plain, since every other schedulable item is named by something a person typed while a class alone borrowed its name from a foreign key. It gains a **required `title`** (1–120 characters, `ar-x-icu` collated per TD-6a, bounded by TD-9) and an **optional `description`** (≤ 2,000 characters — the bound `Event.description` and `EducationalContent.description` already take). **Both are editable after creation**, unlike Subject, target, branch and academic year, which §4.4 freezes because changing them re-points Sessions already materialized against the old answer; **a name is not such a value**, since renaming a class changes nothing about what was taught, to whom, or when. **The title is a LABEL, never an identifier:** it is not unique and takes no part in conflict detection, recurrence, materialization, the R50 split or audience resolution, and `subject_id` remains what a client filters and links by. A **split successor inherits the name**, because it is the same class continued at a later date. Per TD-6b the column arrives **nullable, is backfilled from the Subject's name — which is precisely what those rows already displayed on every screen — and is then set `NOT NULL`**, so no existing schedule changes meaning and nothing is lost.

**Revision 56 (Document Owner decision — one Scheduling page with two views, 2026-08-06):** §14.1's `الجدولة` section listed two nodes, and an administrator had to know whether the thing they wanted to create was an *Event* or a *Course Schedule* **before they could click anything** — a question about the platform's internals, asked at the moment somebody wants to schedule a class. **The two nodes become one screen at `/admin/schedules`**, on which the **type is a field**: `حصة` routes to a Course Schedule, `نشاط` to an Event, and `امتحان` to §4.6's `Exam` when M5 ships. **The models do NOT merge and R51's reasoning is unchanged** — §20 rule 22 stands, Events are still computed on read while Sessions are materialized (TD-4.6c), and §4.4 still computes conflicts against real rows because comparing recurrence rules cannot see that a weekly and a biweekly Tuesday class collide only on alternate weeks. **One screen carries two views**, chosen by a query parameter rather than a second navigation node (§20 rule 16): a **List view of definitions**, where one weekly class is one row rather than forty, and a **Calendar view of occurrences** from `GET /calendar`. That distinction is the substantive one — the two old pages listed *rules* and *expanded occurrences* respectively, which is the reason beneath every visual difference that they never read as one feature. **`GET /events` is added**, because no list of event *definitions* existed: reads had always gone through `GET /calendar`, which returns their expansion. **`Event.type` is deliberately NOT added.** §4.4(6) makes a cancellation an edit to a **Session** row rather than a parallel Event mechanism, and BR-17 keeps non-teaching activity out of the routine timetable — so **a holiday suppresses no class**, and the category would drive no rule, no job and no report; the vocabulary already lives in §4.4, §7, BR-17 and the glossary as prose, and an administrator who means `عطلة` writes it in the title. It may be introduced when filtering or reporting by category becomes a real requirement, with a purpose to justify it. **`/admin/schedules/{id}/sessions` is unchanged** and keeps R50's three edit scopes.

**Revision 55 (Document Owner decisions — dependent selection, split taxonomy nodes, Users columns, recurrence bounds, 2026-08-06):** **(1) Every selector in the platform is dependent** (§14.4): it offers only values valid for the current selection, changing one refreshes those below it, and **a selection no longer offered is cleared rather than kept** — the interface must not be able to express a combination the server will refuse. **Academic Year is exempt**, because the platform's years are global (§4.10) and inventing a dependency to make the set look uniform would misdescribe the model. **(2) §14.1's single *Categories & Subjects* node becomes two**, `/admin/categories` (الفئات) and `/admin/subjects` (المواد); **the implementation stays single** — they are the same kind of record, so the entity is a parameter and the screen is written once. **(3) §14.2's Users table gains `email`** — the bound `UserIdentity` address, or `pre_provisioned_email` for an account not yet claimed (R15), and `null` for a login-less minor student, which is a fact rather than a gap (§4.3) — together with its already-specified **Branch scope** column, which had never been rendered. This is **not** a display identity: §20 rule 21 governs the name shown to the public, while this is an administrative identifier on a staff-only screen (TD-2). **(4) `effective_until` (R50) and `anchor_date` join the course-schedule write contract.** The column existed from R50 and could only be set as a side effect of *splitting* a schedule, so a class running for one term could not be described as running for one term — while an Event, the other half of the same scheduling module, has carried a recurrence end since it shipped; and without `anchor_date` a `biweekly_alternating` class could not say which fortnight was *on* (§7). **Recorded as a defect fixed rather than a decision taken:** `LevelSubject` (§4.4b) — what a Level teaches — was enforced by teaching-group splits and by content uploads but **not by course-schedule creation**, and the two that did enforce it used different reason codes. The live database consequently held Course Schedules whose Levels officially taught nothing, and to which no content could then be attached. One policy now governs all three surfaces, raising **`SUBJECT_NOT_IN_LEVEL`**.

**Revision 53 (content replacement and deletion get a contract, 2026-08-06):** TD-3.5 defined creation and no lifecycle, while TD-9 already specified how replacement must behave and TD-5/BR-15 how deletion must — mechanics with no endpoint to reach them, though §5.5 and §5.6 both list the actions. **Replacement extends the upload flow**: `content_meta` gains an optional `replaces_content_id`, and completion updates that record rather than creating one — **a new key with a new hash segment, the previous object quarantined, `version` incremented**, exactly as TD-9 requires. It is deliberately **not** a route of its own: a replacement *is* an upload, needing the same presigned PUT, the same whitelist and cap checks, the same magic-byte verification and the same quota, and a second route would be that flow written twice. **Deletion is its own route**, `DELETE /content/{id}`, because it moves no bytes in: soft delete, `Trash` snapshot, and **the object moved to `quarantine/…` rather than destroyed**, so BR-15's ninety-day window means for a recording what it means everywhere else. ~~**No permanent-delete route**, consistent with Revision 52.~~ **Superseded by Revision 59.1:** a Super Admin purge exists and also removes the quarantined object.

**Revision 52 (Document Owner decision — the Trash page ships now, with per-entity restore, 2026-08-05):** Revision 6 deferred `/admin/trash` in full and Revision 7 marked that deferral as intentional. **The browsing page returns to the MVP**, and **restore returns per entity type rather than universally** — the Owner's direction: *"do not delay the entire feature because some entity types are more complex; the UI can support different capabilities per entity type."*

**A Super Admin screen** lists every soft-deleted record with its entity type, a label read from the snapshot, who deleted it, when, and when BR-15's **seven-day** window purges it *(Revision 133; ~~ninety-day~~ superseded)*, with filtering by type and date and a search. **Super Admin only** (TD-2): the list spans every entity regardless of branch, which no other surface allows.

**Restore is offered only where restoration is COMPLETE, and the decision is the SERVER'S**, published per row and never inferred by a client — a screen cannot know which deletions cascade, and one that guessed would offer a button that silently half-restores a person. **Restorable today: `Branch`, `Category`, `Subject` and `Room`** — the types whose deletion is **guarded rather than cascading**, so nothing was removed alongside them and clearing the tombstone genuinely restores the whole record. **Every other type is refused loudly** (`409 STATE_CONFLICT`, `reason: CASCADE_RELATIONSHIPS` or `CASCADE_CHILDREN`) and the screen **states the reason** rather than merely hiding the action: §7's hazard is unchanged — *a User restored without their links, enrollments and roles is a half-restored, silently broken account* — and clearing `deleted_at` is the easy tenth of that problem, every failure of the rest being silent. **A type joins the restorable set when its reinstatement is written and tested, not before.**

**Two further guards.** A **child is never restored into a deleted parent** (`PARENT_DELETED`) — a Room whose Branch is still in the Trash would be technically alive and practically unreachable. And a record BR-15 has already purged answers `ALREADY_PURGED`: the snapshot alone cannot safely recreate it, because every foreign key it names may have gone too.

~~**No permanent-delete action exists, and none may be added without a further revision.**~~ **Superseded by Revision 59:** permanent deletion exists as a Super Admin action, `DELETE /admin/trash/{id}`, audited and refused loudly where dependencies remain. BR-15's ninety-day window remains the **default** path and is unchanged — and the `content.quarantine-purge` job this paragraph names as its enforcement **was never built** (Revision 59.4).

**The snapshot is never published on the wire.** It is the row exactly as it was, including columns no screen is entitled to; this page answers *what was deleted, by whom, and when*, and restoration reads the snapshot server-side.

**Revision 51 (Document Owner decision — Scheduling is one navigation section, 2026-08-05):** §14.1 listed Course Schedules under *Academic* and Events under *Calendar*, which follows the **models** rather than the question an administrator is asking. Both are things that happen on a calendar, and finding them in unrelated parts of the menu made them feel like unrelated systems. **They join one section, `الجدولة` (Scheduling), with two nodes — `الأنشطة` (Events) and `الحصص` (Sessions).** **No route changes**, and **nothing about the models changes**: §4.4's separation of Events from teaching occurrences and §20 rule 22's prohibition on re-conflating organisation with delivery both stand. This is a statement about **where a person looks**, not about what the two things are.

**The presentation layer is shared; the models are not.** Both screens render the same page layout, the same table, the same toolbars, the same dialogs, the same field primitives and the same recurrence control. **Three components are deliberately NOT shared, because the interface would otherwise imply a capability the model does not have:** a **room selector** (an Event has no room — §7 gives it no `room_id`), the **occurrence scope dialog** (Revision 50's three scopes need a materialized occurrence to override, and an Event's occurrences are computed on read), and the **conflict preview** (conflicts are room, teacher and assistant overlaps, and an Event has none of the three). Where a control cannot be shared, the screens **omit it rather than disable it** — a dead control teaches nothing (§14.2).

**Revision 50 (Document Owner decision — recurrence edit scopes, 2026-08-05):** an administrator editing, cancelling or deleting one occurrence of a recurring class **must be asked which occurrences the change applies to**, and offered exactly three scopes: **this session only**, **this session and all future sessions**, and **all sessions of this schedule**. The question is mandatory for **every operation that can reach a series**, not only for edits — an administrator who moves "the Tuesday class" without being asked cannot know whether they moved one week or a year.

**Two scopes are unchanged and were already implemented.** *This session only* is `PATCH /sessions/{id}`, which sets `overridden` (Revision 43.4 — *a human decided about this occurrence*) and is thereafter protected from schedule rewrites (Revision 43.6). *All sessions* is `PATCH /admin/course-schedules/{id}`, which rewrites future un-overridden sessions and reports `resynced` and `protected_sessions`.

**"This session and all future" is implemented by SPLITTING the schedule, and no other mechanism is authorised.** The current schedule is closed at the day before the selected occurrence and a **successor schedule** is created carrying the new values, anchored at that occurrence. Past sessions are untouched; overridden sessions keep their overrides; sessions from the split date follow the successor. **An exception model is explicitly rejected:** the platform already materializes every occurrence as a real `Session` (TD-4.6c) and already marks the ones a human touched, so a second way to say *this occurrence differs* would be two representations of one fact — the §20 rule 22 hazard — and §4.4 computes conflicts **against materialized sessions rather than rules**, which a rule-carrying-exceptions would defeat. A split needs no new entity: both halves are ordinary schedules, so conflict detection, rosters, the calendar and the Session page keep working with no knowledge that a split occurred.

**One schema change:** `RecurringCourseSchedule` gains **`effective_until`** (nullable calendar date, TD-11). **`NULL` means open-ended**, which every schedule created before this revision is, and most created after it. Together with `anchor_date` it bounds one series, and materialization gains exactly one condition — stop at `effective_until`, with the earlier of it and the academic-year horizon winning.

**No new endpoint.** The scope travels as a parameter on the existing schedule edit; a second route returning the same resource would be duplication rather than separation (Revision 45).

**Revision 43 (Document Owner decision — the educational model separates organisation from delivery, 2026-08-04):** until this revision a **Group** was four facts in one row: a roster of students, a weekly timetable slot, a room booking, and a teacher assignment. That conflation is why a student could sit in only one slot per Level, why a Level could not have two Subjects taught at different times to differently-split students, and why Revision 39 needed a whole revision to explain that `intended_branch_id` is a *request* rather than a placement. The association's real teaching model — one Level, several Subjects, each Subject split differently, Tafsir taught to everyone at once while Quran runs in three parallel groups — could not be expressed at all.

This revision decomposes that single entity into four, along the seam between **who is organised with whom** and **what is taught, when, by whom, where**.

**(1) The hierarchy is Category → Level → Administrative Group → Enrollment.** An **Administrative Group** is the permanent organisational unit inside a Level: the roster used for organisation, reporting, communication, attendance (§4.7) and the default timetable. It is **not** subject-specific. Creating a Level automatically creates its first Administrative Group (**المجموعة 1**), so a Level is never in a state where a student cannot be admitted to it. Administrative Groups **do not own a room, a teacher, an assistant or a schedule** — those belong to delivery.

**(2) A student belongs to one or more Levels, and to exactly one Administrative Group inside each enrolled Level.** Level membership is **not stored separately** — it is the answer to `Enrollment → AdministrativeGroup → Level`. A separate `StudentLevel` entity was drafted under the number 43 and is **withdrawn**: it would have been a second source of truth for a fact the hierarchy already carries, and the two would drift. The "exactly one group per Level" rule spans two hops, so `Enrollment` carries `level_id` alongside `administrative_group_id` and a **composite foreign key** `(administrative_group_id, level_id) → AdministrativeGroup(id, level_id)` makes the redundancy *provably* consistent rather than merely intended — which then permits a real `UNIQUE (student_id, level_id)`.

**(3) The Administrative Group keeps `branch_id`.** Delivery moves away from it; place does not. An Administrative Group is a roster of people *at a premises*, and this is what keeps "which branch is this person in" answerable with one value — preserving the Revision 39 request-vs-placement distinction, staff branch scoping, and the §4.9 Global-scope authorization derivation. Without it a student's branch would become many-valued (derived from wherever their sessions happen to be) and three separate rules would lose their subject.

**(4) A Teaching Group belongs to one Subject and one Level — never to an Administrative Group.** It exists **only** when a Subject needs students split differently from the administrative roster. A Subject with no Teaching Groups is taught to the whole Level. Teaching Groups are **independent between Subjects**: a student may sit in Administrative Group 1, Quran Group 2 and Tajweed Group 1 simultaneously. A student holds **at most one** Teaching Group per (Subject, Level), and membership is **optional** — but a student enrolled in a Level whose Subject *is* split and who holds no Teaching Group for it appears on an explicit **unassigned** list for that Subject, because a silent gap here is a student with no sessions and nobody noticing.

**(5) A Recurring Course Schedule is the unit of delivery.** It carries the Subject, the **teaching mode**, the Branch, the Room, the Teacher, the Assistant(s), a start and end time, and a recurrence rule. **Teaching mode belongs to the schedule and to nothing else** — never to a Subject, a Level or a student: Tafsir is taught to an entire Level while Quran, *in that same Level*, runs in Teaching Groups, so mode cannot be a property of either. The three modes are **Entire Level**, **Administrative Group**, and **Teaching Group**, and a schedule targets **exactly one** entity of the named kind. Plural targeting was considered and rejected: it buys nothing a second schedule row does not, and it would make the roster query, the room-conflict check and the consent gate each take a set where a single value suffices.

**(6) Sessions are materialized from schedules by a background job, and are individually overridable.** Each Session carries its own date, time, room, teacher and status, defaulted from its schedule. This is what makes a room change, a cancellation, a reschedule and a makeup class **edits to a Session row** rather than a parallel Event mechanism — and it is why materialization is eager rather than computed on read: conflict detection then runs against real rows and is *exact*, where comparing recurrence rules cannot see that a weekly and a bi-weekly Tuesday 15:00 collide only on alternate weeks. §10.1 has named `session_cancelled` and `session_rescheduled` as critical notification events since Revision 6; this revision gives them the entity they always presupposed.

**(7) `Event` remains, narrowed to non-teaching activity.** Holidays, ceremonies, exams, one-off activities, vacations. It keeps its visibility tiers, its recurrence and its four-way scope joins. **The former sentence "Scheduling is Group-driven, not Event-driven" is retired** — scheduling is now schedule-driven, and Events never generate Sessions.

**(8) Teacher scope resolves through the Course Schedule, not through `GroupTeacher`.** §7 previously called `GroupTeacher` "the resolution table for ALL teacher scoping", and that sentence fed TD-2, Hidden-event visibility, sensitive social-data access, Quran logging and exam authoring. It is replaced by **`CourseScheduleStaff (schedule_id, user_id, position)`**, which also **generalises the existing two-slot co-teaching rule instead of duplicating it** and gives assistants a home without inventing a Role (§7: roles are seeded, with no CRUD in the MVP). This is also strictly better: `schedule.branch_id` states a teacher's branch scope directly, where the old model inferred it through two hops. **The derivation is stated once, in §4.4c, and every other section points at it.**

**(9) Quran becomes a Subject — for scheduling only.** §4.4b previously said the Quran is *never* a Subject. That rule was about **tracking**, and it stays true of tracking: memorization is recorded by Surah and ayah range through the §4.5 engine, and `LevelSurah` remains the Quran-side curriculum join. But a timetable must be able to say *"this session is Quran"*, and the specification had no way to express it. Quran may therefore be a `Subject` and carry Teaching Groups and schedules, with **an explicit BR-9 carve-out: a Quran `LevelSubject` auto-generates no grading components** — the Adult-stage components already come from `LevelSurah` (§4.6), and generating both would create two competing sets for one body of work.

**(10) Approval assigns Levels and Administrative Groups.** A pending request is approved by selecting **one or more Levels**, with the first Level of the applicant's Category preselected — a default the administrator may keep, remove or add to. For each selected Level the administrator chooses **one Administrative Group**, auto-selected when the Level has only one. **Teaching Groups are never assigned at approval**; they are assigned later, when a particular Subject is organised, because at approval nobody yet knows how each Subject will be split.

**(11) Room capacity is informational and is never enforced.** `Room.capacity` is displayed to assist planning; no business logic refuses an assignment on it. The Administrative Group carries **no capacity at all** — it has no room to be constrained by. This deletes `Group.max_students`, its CHECK constraint, and the only roster-side `SELECT … FOR UPDATE` discipline in TD-15.2. Administrators remain responsible for assigning suitable rooms; the platform surfaces the number and does not pretend to know better.

**(12) The Calendar and the Educational Library are both public.** Anonymous visitors browse both without authenticating, and **the available filters are identical for anonymous and authenticated users**. For a signed-in person the filters are **prefilled** from their profile (a student's own Category, enrolled Levels and Branch; a teacher's branches, subjects and sessions; a parent's children; an administrator's scope) and remain freely changeable. **Identical filters never means identical results:** the §4.4 three-tier visibility model still filters every result set, and a public Teacher filter is a public surface — it renders the `display_name` the API resolved, never a legal name (§20 rule 21).

> **Revision 135 clarification:** the paragraph above is preserved as Revision 43 history. Its automatic-prefill expectation for the public `/calendar` is superseded by §4.4: profile suggestions do not automatically populate its controls. Public access, visibility enforcement and the Educational Library rules are unchanged; personal calendars retain Revision 82(8)'s audience scoping.

**(13) Educational Content is owned by Level, Subject and Academic Year, with an optional Branch.** `subject_id` becomes **required** alongside the existing required `level_id`; a null `branch_id` still means Global (§4.9). Deriving the Level from the Subject was rejected: a Subject spans several Levels, so the derivation is many-valued and *"which Level is this for"* would stop having an answer. **Content is not owned by Sessions** — a Session *references* content, many-to-many, so one semester PDF can be referenced by every session that uses it, and content outlives the session that first linked it.

**(14) The consent gate is restated on the session's resolved audience.** BR-2/BR-3 previously read *"if a Group has even one enrolled student without effective `media_release` consent, all session-recording resources for that Group are forced private"*. With three teaching modes that sentence has no subject. It becomes: **the students the session's audience resolves to** — the Level's students at that branch, or the Administrative Group's, or the Teaching Group's. One rule, all three modes, and §4.9's zero-student edge case keeps its analogue: a session resolving to no students has no non-consenting student, so the gate does not engage.

**(15) Attendance is specified now and built later.** §4.7 stops saying only *"postponed"* and states the model: attendance is recorded **per Session, per student**, against the session's resolved audience. **No table, endpoint, screen or job is created in the MVP** — §20 rule 16 stands. Specifying it now is what stops the Session entity from being designed in a way that cannot carry it. **Announcements** receive the same treatment: they will attach to Sessions, and nothing is built now.

**Revision 43.1 (Document Owner adjustment, 2026-08-04):** Administrative Group 1 is created **immediately with its Level**, from a Branch supplied on the create form, rather than lazily when a Branch first appears. The Branch is an *input* to Level creation and is **not** a column on `Level` — a Level stays Category-scoped and branch-independent, and may hold groups at several Branches. One bounded exception remains, because §15.1 seeds Levels and forbids seeding Branches: **the first Branch created in a deployment backfills المجموعة 1 for every Level that has none.** That is a bootstrap completion step running once, not a standing lazy rule. A consequence worth stating: a deployment whose owner has not yet created a Branch cannot approve a registration — correctly, since there is nowhere to place the applicant.

**Revision 43.2 (implementation correction, 2026-08-04):** Revision 43 specified the at-most-one-Teaching-Group-per-Subject rule as *"a functional unique index over the join"*. **That is not expressible in PostgreSQL** — a unique index cannot reference another table — and the defect surfaced on the first attempt to write the migration. `StudentTeachingGroup` therefore carries `subject_id` and `level_id` **on the row**, constrained by a **composite foreign key** to `TeachingGroup(id, subject_id, level_id)`: the identical technique `Enrollment` uses, for the identical reason. `TeachingGroup` gains the redundant `UNIQUE (id, subject_id, level_id)` that FK needs as its target. **The rule is unchanged; only its mechanism is.** Recorded as a revision rather than silently substituted, because a reader comparing the schema against Revision 43's wording would otherwise find a discrepancy and no explanation.

**Revision 43.3 (contradiction found during implementation, 2026-08-04):** Revision 43 granted Admins `✔ (own branches)` over *"Manage Teaching Groups and their membership"* (TD-2) while defining a Teaching Group as carrying **only** `subject_id` and `level_id` (§7, §4.4c) — and a Level is explicitly branch-independent (§4.4b). **"Own branches" therefore had no referent**, and the scope check every other operational service performs could not be written at all. The concrete failure it permitted: a Marrakesh Admin creating, renaming or **deleting** the Quran split for a Level whose students also sit at Targa, while the unassigned list showed them only Marrakesh students — authority over everyone, visibility of some. **Resolution: the row splits in two.** **Teaching Group CRUD is Super Admin only**, joining the Levels and Subjects it organises under Revision 26's rule that curriculum *structure* is reference data. **Membership stays an Admin action**, scoped by the branch the student is actually enrolled at (`Enrollment → AdministrativeGroup.branch_id`) — a referent that exists. Rejected: deriving a scope from "this Admin runs a group in this Level" (it keeps two Admins jointly authoritative over one Level's splits, which is the hazard rather than a fix), and adding `branch_id` to `TeachingGroup` (the most domain-accurate answer, since students do not commute between branches — recorded here as the option to revisit if branch-local splits are ever needed, and deliberately not taken while no Teaching Group rows exist to migrate). *(Taken by Revision 172 §15, at the Owner's word, with the authority split kept.)*

**Revision 43.4 (Document Owner decision — a Session snapshots its teaching assignment, 2026-08-04):** Revision 43 gave a Session *its own room and teacher* but never said **when** those are written, and the first implementation left the teacher null and re-derived staffing from the schedule. **That makes history mutable:** changing a schedule's teacher in June would silently re-attribute March's classes to someone who never taught them. **Room and staff are now snapshot onto the Session at materialization**, and the single `teacher_id` is **replaced by `SessionStaff (session_id, user_id, position)`** — mirroring `CourseScheduleStaff`, because the association co-teaches and classes have assistants, and because one place must answer *"who taught this"* or the two drift.

**The consequent rule, which is the point of the change:** a Session that has already happened — a past date, or any Session in `held` or `cancelled` — **keeps what it was materialized with, whatever the schedule later says.** A schedule edit reaches only future, un-overridden Sessions, where it now updates room, staff and times rather than merely creating missing dates. *(That re-sync was a genuine gap: the first implementation created and deleted occurrences but never refreshed the ones already there, so §4.4's promise that an edit "rewrites future Sessions" was not actually true.)* **Bringing history back into line with a schedule is an explicit, audited administrator action**, never a side effect.

**This does not contradict §20 rule 22's ban on snapshotting a roster.** The *audience* must resolve live because enrolment changes; *staffing* records what was arranged for one occurrence. The two rules describe opposite kinds of fact, which is why both exist.

**Revision 43.5 (Document Owner rule — a Session carrying educational work is protected, 2026-08-04):** Revision 43 protected an `overridden` Session and one carrying attached content; Revision 43.4 protected everything in the past. This closes the remaining case: **a FUTURE Session that already carries educational work** — attendance, grades, recordings, notes, homework, attached content — **must never be silently regenerated by a schedule update either.** The protection is **date-independent**, because the work exists now whatever the date says: a recording attached to next Tuesday's class is exactly as much someone's labour as one attached to last Tuesday's.

**Regeneration of a protected Session requires explicit administrator confirmation, naming the sessions.** Not a flag on the edit, not a side effect, and deliberately **no blanket "regenerate all" option** — an option that can be defaulted true is not a confirmation. The administrator sees what was left alone and why, then names what they want overwritten. Admin-only, audited with what it overwrote.

**"Carries work" is ONE predicate.** As each kind of work ships — attendance (§4.7), notes and announcements (§10.1) — it joins that predicate and every caller inherits the protection automatically. Stating it here rather than in each call site is the point: a second copy of the test is precisely how a future feature would silently lose it.

**Revision 43.6 (Document Owner direction — session protection is a semantic rule with one extensibility point, 2026-08-04):** Revisions 43 through 43.5 protected sessions by naming the things that protect them — overridden, held, cancelled, has attached content. Left alone, that predicate becomes **a hardcoded list of features that every new module must remember to join**, and the module that forgets does not fail loudly; it silently loses a safeguard.

**The rule is restated semantically:** *a Session is protected whenever it holds data created by a user or an administrator whose loss or silent modification would change historical truth.* Attendance, grades, educational content, evaluations, certificates, messaging and behavioural reports are **instances** of that rule, not clauses of it. The test an implementer applies is *"would losing this misrepresent what happened?"* — a question about the domain, answerable without reading the scheduling code.

**And it has exactly one mechanism.** Every scheduling operation asks it, and a module **contributes a condition** to it while knowing nothing about schedules, sessions-in-general, or how regeneration works. Two properties are required of that mechanism and are not negotiable: contributions are **evaluated in bulk**, so protection never becomes a per-session query, and the **built-in conditions are always present** rather than registered at boot — a protection that can be switched off by forgetting to call a bootstrap function is not a protection.

**Scope consequence:** this re-opens M3 (Scheduling & Calendar), which was complete and green, and inserts a new milestone **before M4** — Quran progress and Exams both resolve students through the group model, and building them against the old shape would mean writing them twice. §8, §9 and `docs/IMPLEMENTATION_PLAN.md` are re-sequenced accordingly.

**Revision 42 (Document Owner decision — Platform Settings becomes a real, audited surface, 2026-08-02):** *(Its subject, `legal.consent_text_version`, is **superseded by Revision 119**, which replaces the free-text setting with `LegalConsentText` — a version and its exact wording as one immutable record. The endpoints, the allow-list mechanism, the audit rule and the TD-15 locking below all stand; the key they were built for has left the allow-list, and R119 states why. The gap this revision closed is closed by R119's own surface.)* closes a gap that made the platform undeployable. `legal.consent_text_version` is **required** before any registration can be accepted (§4.1a), §15.1 deliberately does not seed it (§2.3 makes versioning the Arabic text an owner compliance task), and the screen meant to carry it — **System Settings (`/superadmin/settings`, §5.6)** — had **no API behind it in TD-3**. The value could therefore be set by nothing in the product: not by an operator, not by an administrator, not by the seed. A first deployment would have refused every registration with no in-product remedy, and the interim answer — a development fixture — is **not** an acceptable production mechanism.

**(1) Two endpoints join TD-3.11.** `GET /admin/settings` and `PUT /admin/settings/{key}`. **Super Admin only**, asserted against live rows per request (TD-12) — §5.6 places System Settings under Super Admin, and a consent text version decides what every future applicant is recorded as having agreed to.

**(2) The writable surface is an explicit allow-list, not "any key".** A `SystemSetting` row can hold a category default visibility, a grading scale, or a legal text version, and those have different audiences and different consequences. Only keys named in the allow-list may be written through this API; anything else is `NOT_FOUND`, so a typo creates nothing and an unlisted key cannot be reached. **`legal.consent_text_version` is the first entry**; the rest join as their screens do.

**(3) A value is validated per key and may never be empty.** For `legal.consent_text_version`: a non-empty string after trimming, 1–100 characters. Blank, whitespace-only and non-string values are refused with `VALIDATION_FAILED` — a blank version would reproduce the original failure while *looking* configured, which is worse than being unset.

**(4) Every write is audited (TD-8), carrying the OLD and NEW value.** `setting.update`, target `SystemSetting`, keyed by the setting. The previous value is part of the record because the question a compliance reviewer asks is *"what did this person agree to, and when did that text change"* — an audit row saying only the new value cannot answer it.

**(5) Changing the setting affects FUTURE registrations only, and this is normative.** Existing `ConsentRecord` rows keep the `consent_text_version` captured at the moment of agreement; the setting is read **at registration time** and copied onto the record. **A settings change never rewrites a stored consent.** §4.1a's requirement that each record carry the exact text agreed to is precisely what forbids it: retroactively restamping consents would assert that people agreed to text they never saw.

**(6) TD-15 optimistic locking.** `SystemSetting` already carries `version`; a stale write is `409 VERSION_CONFLICT` rather than a silent overwrite of another Super Admin's change.

**Consequence:** the development fixture that seeded this value is no longer the mechanism — it remains only a convenience for local work, and a production deployment sets the value through the screen like any other configuration.

**Revision 41 (Document Owner decision — the French name is split like the Arabic one, 2026-08-02):** Revision 40 split the Arabic name into **الاسم الشخصي** and **الاسم العائلي** and explicitly left the French name as a single optional field, noting that splitting it would be a separate revision. This is that revision: **`first_name_french` and `last_name_french` replace `name_french`**, for one reason — a person has one name, and recording half of it as two fields and half as one is an inconsistency every screen and every export would have to reason about.

The design mirrors Revision 40 exactly, deliberately, so there is one rule rather than two: the **parts are collected**, `name_french` is **retained and server-composed** as `"{first} {last}"`, a client never composes it (§1.1), and the parts are **nullable with no backfill** — splitting an existing French name on whitespace would be the same guess Revision 40 refused to make for Arabic. **The French name remains entirely optional**, unlike the Arabic one: an applicant may give both parts or neither, and giving exactly one is a validation failure, because half a name is not a name.

**Revision 40 (Document Owner decision — the Arabic name is captured as personal + family name, 2026-08-02):** the registration form asked for a single *"full name in Arabic"* while presenting the person's name as one opaque string. The association records people the way Moroccan administrative practice does — **الاسم الشخصي** (personal name) and **الاسم العائلي** (family name) — and a single field cannot be sorted, searched or addressed by either half without guessing where one ends.

**(1) Two new fields on `User` (§7): `first_name_arabic` and `last_name_arabic`.** Both are captured for **every person the registration transaction creates** — the applicant, and the child on the parent+child path — in the same transaction that creates them (§4.1b step 5), never patched on afterwards. They are the *collected* facts.

**(2) `name_arabic` is retained and becomes a SERVER-COMPOSED value.** It is *not* removed and it is *not* asked for. The server writes `"{first} {last}"` in the same transaction, and it remains what every existing consumer reads: TD-10's normalized search columns, the `ar-x-icu` ordering that BR-19 requires, §7's public display-identity fallback, and the §14.2 approval queue. **A client never composes it** (§1.1): two clients would disagree about order and separator, and the wrong answer would be a person's name rendered backwards. It is a derived column of exactly the same kind as `name_arabic_normalized`, written by the same transaction as its inputs, and it is a defect for the three to disagree.

**Why compose rather than replace.** Replacing `name_arabic` everywhere would touch search, collation, display identity, the approval contract, both seeds and every screen that shows a person — for no gain, because the composed name is genuinely what those consumers want. Keeping it is the expand half of expand–migrate–contract (TD-6b): a contract phase may later drop it, and nothing here forecloses that.

**(3) Nullable, with no backfill.** Accounts created before this revision have a `name_arabic` and no parts, and **splitting an existing full name on whitespace would be a guess** — Moroccan names routinely carry compound personal names and multi-word family names, so a split would silently mis-file people. A null means *this name was recorded before the parts were collected*, and `name_arabic` remains authoritative for those rows. **Required in the public self-service registration payload** (§4.1b step 5), where the applicant is present to type them; **optional for staff-assisted registration** (§4.1), where staff may hold only a full name.

**(4) TD-9 limits.** Each part is 1–60 characters, so the composed `name_arabic` cannot exceed the 120 its column already allows. The French name is **unchanged** — a single optional `name_french`; this revision does not split it, and any future decision to do so is a separate revision.

**(5) Nothing else changes.** No new endpoint, no change to who may register, no change to placement (Revision 39 stands), and no change to the display-identity invariant — `public_display_name` still overrides the composed name on public surfaces, and the client still renders what the server resolved (§20 rule 21).

**Revision 39 (Document Owner decision — registration captures the applicant's chosen Branch, 2026-08-01):** corrects the specification against the association's real intake process. **Revision 29's "registration never places a beneficiary" is superseded in part**: the workflow it described — an applicant who chooses nothing, and staff who assign everything after approval — is not how بذور الأمل actually receives people. The real sequence is: *applicant → **chooses the Branch** → registration request created → administrator reviews and approves/rejects → **after approval**, the administrator assigns the applicant to a **Group***.

**How this was found, and why it is a specification defect rather than an implementation one.** Building the §14.2 Approvals screen surfaced a filter — *"Filters: Type, Branch"* — with no data behind it. An end-to-end trace established that the value was not collected-and-lost but **never collected**: the registration payload schema rejected `branch_id` outright, no column existed on `User` or `FamilyLink`, and neither approval query had a branch in scope. Two readings were possible: §14.2 was wrong, or Revisions 25 and 29 were. **The Document Owner confirms the latter** — §14.2 recorded the real process, and Revision 29 wrote down a workflow the association does not use. The implementation was faithful to the document throughout; the document was wrong.

**(1) The applicant chooses exactly one organisational value: Branch.** Registration captures **Branch and nothing else** — **no Level, no Room, no Group**. Those remain administrative decisions taken after approval, and the prohibition on an applicant selecting them is **retained verbatim from Revision 29**. This revision narrows that prohibition by exactly one field; it does not open the door to the others, and an implementing agent must not add them (§20 rule 20).

**(2) The captured value is a REQUEST, not a placement — and the distinction is normative.** `User.intended_branch_id` (§7) records **the branch the applicant asked for**. It is **not** the authoritative answer to *"which branch is this person in"*: that answer remains the person's **Group**, through `StudentGroup → Group.branch_id`, and an administrator may legitimately place an approved applicant in a Group at a different branch when the requested one is full or unsuitable. **Naming it `branch_id` would have created a second source of truth for one concept**, which is the failure §16.4 exists to prevent and which every duplicated requirement in this document's history has demonstrated. The two facts are genuinely different — what was asked for, and where the person ended up — and they are stored, named and read as two facts.

**(3) Nullable, and required only where it is genuinely available.** The column is **nullable**, because accounts predating this revision have no such value and **no backfill is possible** — nobody knows what those applicants would have chosen, and inventing a branch for them would be worse than leaving the truth absent. It is **required in the public self-service registration payload** (§4.1b step 5), because that is where the applicant is present to choose. It stays **optional for staff-assisted registration** (§4.1), where staff may be recording a person whose branch is not yet decided. A null therefore means *not stated*, never *no branch*.

**(4) The branch must be real, open, and not deleted.** The server validates `intended_branch_id` against a live `Branch` — a soft-deleted branch is refused, exactly as the public directory refuses to advertise closed premises (Revision 35). A branch whose `operational_start_date` has not yet occurred is nonetheless **selectable**: §4.4 excludes such a branch from the *calendar*, but an association opening a new premises must be able to take registrations for it before it opens, which is the whole point of recording an opening date.

**(5) The approval queue exposes the branch and §14.2's Branch filter becomes implementable.** `GET /admin/approvals` carries `branch` (`{ id, name }` or `null`) on every item and accepts a `branch_id` filter. **The queue's visibility is deliberately NOT scoped by branch** — Revision 25's and Revision 29's statement that it is *"a separate surface, not scoped by this rule"* is **retained and remains binding**, because a branch Admin must still be able to see an applicant whose chosen branch is wrong, or absent, in order to correct it. §14.2 specifies a **filter**, which is a reader's chosen narrowing, and this revision implements exactly that and no more. **Turning the filter into a scope would be an access-control change and requires its own revision.**

**(6) A family-link item has no branch, and shows none.** A standalone §4.3 link request concerns an existing child whose placement already lives in their Group; the request itself carries no branch, so its `branch` is `null` and a `branch_id` filter does not match it. This is stated because the alternative — resolving a link item's branch through the student's enrolment — would make one filter mean two different things depending on the row it was applied to.

**(7) Consequences elsewhere, all retained.** Approval still grants no placement: `decide()` sets `account_status` and decides links, and **creates no role assignment and no enrolment** (TD-4.2 unchanged). The §14.2 **user-list** visibility rule (Revision 25) is **unchanged** — it turns on a live *role assignment*, and an intended branch is not one, so a self-registered applicant remains Super-Admin-visible in that list until staff attach them to a branch. Revision 29's separation of **registration metadata from reference data** is **unchanged and reaffirmed**: the registration form obtains branches from the existing public `GET /branches` (TD-3.9, Revision 35), **not** from a registration-metadata endpoint that would duplicate reference data behind a public surface.

**Revision 38 (Document Owner decision — the API contract is an intentional interface, never an accidental serialisation of database models, 2026-08-01):** closes a defect found while building the first CRUD screen and generalises the rule that prevents it recurring.

**The defect.** `GET /admin/branches` returned **raw Prisma rows**: row fields in `camelCase` while its `meta` envelope was `snake_case` — two conventions in one response — `operational_start_date` as a **datetime** where TD-11 makes a branch's operational start a **date**, and four internal columns (`created_at`, `updated_at`, `deleted_at`, `deleted_by`) shipped to every caller. Every other endpoint returns an explicit `snake_case` projection; the public `GET /branches` is the **same entity in the other convention**. The frontend had absorbed the difference in an adapter, which is the wrong repair: **adapters exist to adapt a contract to a UI model, not to compensate for an inconsistent contract.**

**The rule, now binding (§16.2).** **No endpoint may expose an ORM entity directly. Every endpoint returns an explicit contract DTO built by an allow-list projection.** The API contract is an **intentional interface**; a response that is whatever the ORM happened to select is an accident that clients then depend on.

**Three consequences, each load-bearing.** *(a)* **A column added to a model must never appear in a response by default.** Revision 35 established this for the public branch directory — *"an endpoint that returns everything except what we remembered to strip is one careless `select` away from leaking"* — and the reasoning was never specific to public endpoints: a staff endpoint leaking `deleted_by` is not a privacy breach, but it is still a contract nobody designed. *(b)* **`snake_case` is the single wire convention**, matching the field names TD-3 already uses throughout. *(c)* **Dates that TD-11 defines as calendar dates are serialised as `YYYY-MM-DD`**, never as instants — an instant invites a timezone conversion in a client, which is the precise class of bug TD-11 exists to prevent.

**Scope of this revision.** The rule is general and applies to every endpoint, present and future. Two current surfaces are brought into compliance: `GET|POST|PATCH /admin/branches` and `/admin/branches/{id}/rooms` (the ORM leak), and `GET /admin/approvals`, which returned a hand-built shape in `camelCase` rather than an ORM model — a convention violation rather than a leak, corrected in the same pass because the alternative is a contract that is *mostly* consistent.

**Enforcement.** A CI guard fails the build where a controller returns a service result without projecting it (§19.2), so the rule holds by construction rather than by review.

**Revision 37.2 (Document Owner decision — the completion-report structure is a mandatory content of the agent files, 2026-07-30):** the second of the two durability pins, and the same shape as Revision 37.1. Completion reports now follow **six sections, in order: user-visible changes · engineering highlights · documentation updates · additional defects discovered · verification · remaining work.** The convention was adopted in `CLAUDE.md` and the handbook; §16.3's mandatory-contents list did not require it, so it would have survived today and **not a future rewrite of the file.**

**What is normative here is the *structure*, not the prose.** The Document Owner reads reports in order to **decide**, so *"what changed for the people using this"*, *"what did it cost"* and *"what is still open"* must be in fixed places rather than distributed through prose at the author's discretion. The wording, the per-section guidance and the rationale stay in the handbook (`docs/development/README.md`) — this document states the requirement, §16.4's tier split states why the *how* lives there, and duplicating the guidance through the SRS would contradict the revision that established that split.

**Two of the six sections carry obligations this document already imposes**, which is what raises the structure above a formatting preference. **Additional defects discovered** is where §16.4's rule that discovered knowledge is technical debt *paid immediately* becomes **visible** rather than buried in a diff that only a line-by-line reviewer would catch — the calendar task alone produced three such fixes, including an accessibility defect that had been latent since the shared dialog was written. **Remaining work** is what keeps a reduction in scope the **Document Owner's decision** instead of an implementer's silence, which is exactly the failure §20 rule 20 guards against in the specification's own domain.

**Applies to `/AGENTS.md` as well**, because an agent that never reads `CLAUDE.md` would otherwise report in whatever shape it chose, and the point of a fixed structure is that it is not per-author.

**Revision 37.1 (Document Owner decision — reading the documentation first is a mandatory content of `CLAUDE.md`, 2026-07-29):** one line, for one reason: **durability.** The Revision-37 work established a per-task workflow whose first step is to *read the relevant project documentation before starting implementation, and determine which documents the task affects*. That step was added to `CLAUDE.md`, but §16.3's mandatory-contents list did not require it — so it would have survived today and not a future rewrite of the file.

**Scope, deliberately narrow.** This adds the **requirement** to §16.3's list and nothing else. The **process** — the before/during/after sequence, the routing of incidental knowledge, the six-condition Definition of Done — stays in the handbook (`docs/development/documentation-policy.md`) under §16.4, because §16.4's tier split is exactly that this document states *what must be true* and the handbook states *how*. Duplicating the procedure through the SRS would contradict the revision that established the split.

**Why the step earns a normative line.** It is the cheapest defence against the failure this document has recorded five times: a second authoritative home for one concept. Reading first is what locates the existing home *before* a duplicate is created — and it is also what surfaces a decision already argued and recorded, which is how a reflex gets rejected rather than re-taken under deadline.

**Revision 37 (Document Owner decision — documentation maintenance is binding, and §16.3 stops carrying verbatim copies, 2026-07-29):** the project documentation was reorganised into a hierarchy under `docs/` (indexed by `docs/README.md`), with a byte-exact pre-restructure snapshot retained under `docs/archive/`. That work established a working rule the Document Owner has adopted as **binding**, and it exposed a drift this revision closes.

**(1) Documentation maintenance becomes a normative obligation — new §16.4.** *A feature is not complete until the documentation describing it is updated, in the same commit as the change.* Previously the only documentation obligation was the `CHANGES.log` ledger row (§16.3), which records **what was done** but not **how the system now works** — so the explanatory documentation could rot while every gate stayed green. Documentation drift is now a **defect**, stated once in §16.4 and cross-referenced everywhere else. §16.4 also fixes the **precedence** question the new hierarchy raises: this SRS remains normative and says what the system *must* do; the `docs/` handbook is explanatory and says *why, how and where*, and it **cites this document rather than restating it**. A handbook page that contradicts the SRS is a defect in the page.

**(2) §16.3 no longer reproduces `CLAUDE.md` and `AGENTS.md` verbatim.** It carried a **full copy** of each file's text. Those copies existed to bootstrap the files at M0, and once the files were committed the copies became exactly the hazard this document has been bitten by repeatedly — **a requirement with two homes drifts, and the copy that drifts still passes every check.** They had already drifted: the live files gained the §16.4 obligation while §16.3 still showed the earlier text. §16.3 now states, normatively, **what each file must contain**; the files themselves carry the wording. This is the same correction Revision 33 item (4) made for TD-15's version list and Revision 36.2 made for the display-identity rule — **a cross-reference, never a second copy.**

**Nothing is weakened by this.** The SRS still pins every requirement those files must state, and §20 rule 20 already makes them derived artifacts that never override this document. What changes is that their *prose* has one home instead of two. The `CHANGES.log` initial-state template is **retained** in §16.3, because unlike the other two it is a genuine one-time bootstrap artifact and no live file can drift from it.

**Recorded for the next reader:** the general lesson is now stated three times in this document's history (Revisions 33, 36.2, 37) and it is the same lesson each time. When an audit finds one requirement in two places, the fix is to delete a copy and link to the survivor — not to synchronise them.

**Revision 36.2 (Document Owner decision — public display identity is an invariant, not an endpoint detail, 2026-07-29):** Revision 36.1 introduced the rule while describing one payload, which left it looking like a property of the calendar. It is not. It is a **platform-wide invariant** and is now stated as one, beside the §7 Attribution invariant it most resembles: **wherever a person's identity is exposed on any public-facing surface — the calendar, event details, educational content, announcements, certificates, and every public surface not yet built — the backend resolves the display identity, and clients render what they are given.**

**The generalisation is the substance.** A rule scoped to one endpoint gets re-decided at the next one, and the second decision is made by whoever is building that screen, under deadline, without the context that produced the first. This is the class of rule where that goes badly: the wrong branch publishes a person's legal name where she asked for a kunya, and nothing in the interface reveals the mistake to the person it affects.

**Duplication removed rather than added.** The rule now has exactly **one** statement — the invariant in §7.
